power_id text
1;2 The study formed part of a larger longitudinal project examining the effects of incarceration on executive functioning, decision-making, and social cognition. Given the substantial attrition typically observed in longitudinal prison research (Dirkzwager & Nieuwbeerta, 2018; Gonçalves et al., 2016; Hassan et al., 2011; Lennox et al., 2013), large baseline samples were targeted. Specifically, the project aimed to recruit approximately 300 incoming prisoners, 300 outgoing prisoners, and 60 community controls at baseline. The target sample size was informed by several a priori power analyses conducted using G*Power (Faul et al., 2007). First, analyses focusing exclusively on incarcerated participants used a mixed repeated-measures design with two groups (incoming vs. outgoing) and six repeated measurements, corresponding to the combination of the three-level Session factor and the two-level Feedback factor (win vs. loss). Assuming a medium effect size (f = .25) and 95% statistical power, the required sample size was estimated at 28 participants (14 per group). Second, analyses including the community control group to evaluate practice effects used a mixed repeated-measures design with three groups and four repeated measurements, corresponding to the two-level Session factor and two-level Feedback factor. Under identical assumptions, the required sample size was estimated at 45 participants (15 per group).
3 Finally, because the broader project focused primarily on longitudinal within-person change across three assessment waves, an additional power analysis was conducted separately within each detainee group. Assuming three sessions and two experimental conditions, this analysis indicated a required sample size of 28 participants per detainee group. Consequently, substantially larger baseline samples were recruited to ensure adequate statistical power following expected attrition.
4;5 The preregistered sample-size target was derived from an a priori power analysis in GPower (medium effect, f = .25, 95% power). However, GPower approximates such designs as repeated-measures ANOVAs and misspecifies interactions that combine between-and within-subject factors (Brysbaert, 2019), and confirmatory analyses used (generalised) linear mixed-effects models. Therefore, an additional simulation-based power analysis for the design actually used was conducted. This analysis was not preregistered and was carried out after data collection. To avoid circularity, the effect size was set a priori by convention rather than from the observed estimates. For each candidate sample size, datasets were simulated from a binomial mixed-effects model with the full Group × Session × Feedback structure, a by-participant random intercept, and random slopes for Session and Feedback. Each participant contributed 125 trials per session, aggregated to binomial counts within each participant × session × feedback cell. The highestorder interaction was tested with a likelihood-ratio test comparing the full model against a model retaining all lower-order (two-way) interactions, and power was defined as the proportion of 500 simulated datasets in which this interaction was detected at α = .05. The cohort design evaluated included three groups × two sessions. Assuming a small interaction (odds ratio = 1.5) and moderate between-participant variability in the session change (random-slope SD = 0.4 on the logit scale), 85% power for the highest-order interaction was reached at approximately 30 participants per group. Main effects and lower-order interactions reached 80% power at smaller samples. The achieved samples met this target for incoming participants (n = 61 with complete behavioural data; n = 46 with usable EEG) and for controls (n = 42 and n = 31, respectively), but fell below it for outgoing participants (n = 25 and n = 19, respectively). Because the outgoing group was under-powered, its posterior contrasts are correspondingly wide, and null or near-zero effects in this group are interpreted with caution rather than as evidence of absence.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 12 imprecise p values out of 60 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

To check (4)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 27 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

For educational material on preventing the misinterpretation of p values, see Improving Your Statistical Inferences.

Missing Files

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. No absolute file paths were found in any of the code files.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In 12 code files, libraries/imports were at multiple places (i.e., with more than 3 non-comment lines in between).

Packages / Dependencies

The code files load 22 distinct packages/libraries: broom.mixed, car, dplyr, emmeans, ggdist, ggplot2, ggsignif, hBayesDM, HDInterval, knitr, lme4, lmerTest, loo, openxlsx, performance, purrr, readr, readxl, rstan, stringr, tibble, tidyr. Reproducible Environment

No renv.lock file, sessionInfo()/session_info() record, or groundhog/checkpoint date-pin call was found anywhere in the repository. Without one of these, the exact R and package versions used for the analysis are not recoverable, and package versions may drift between when this analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt. This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 132 columns across 8 data files.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 8 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

Files are missing from your deposit.

  • 02_eeg_FRN_theta_delta.R reads AGE_IN__see_README.csv.

The script stops at the line that reads the file.

  1. Find the file on your computer.
  2. Add it to the deposit, in the folder where the script looks for it.
  3. If you cannot share it, for example for privacy reasons, say so in the README, and explain where readers can get it and where to put it.

A script uses something it does not create or load itself.

  • 03_sensitivity_gender.R: Participant_number

Usually the object was still in your R session from earlier work, or another script made it.

  1. Restart R (in RStudio: Session > Restart R).
  2. Run each script from top to bottom, and fix what is missing.
  3. If a script needs results of another, save them to a file in the first script and read them in the second.
  4. Load every package with library() at the top.

A script runs longer than our limit of 5 minutes.

  • 00_power_analysis.R
  • 01_behaviour_optimal_choice.R

State in the README how long each script runs. If some steps take hours, save their results (such as fitted models) to files, add those to the deposit, and let the script read them, so readers can check the rest without waiting.

Metacheck can’t automatically establish what caused the error, or how it should be improved.

  • 01_fit_models_control.R
  • 01_fit_models_incoming.R
  • 01_fit_models_outgoing.R

The error message of each script is in the table above.

All details of the Reproducibility Check

This module assesses whether the paper’s code could be run on its data, and — because execute = TRUE — actually ran it. We examined 8 R code files.

File paths

Every data path in the code works in the repository as deposited. 3 data paths were built at runtime with sprintf()/paste()/file.path() instead of written as a literal path — this is fragile authoring practice (it silently breaks if the code is run from a different working directory than the author’s own). We resolved all of these on a best-effort basis.

Execution

The code was run. Each script ran in an isolated Docker container against a throwaway copy of the repository, in the run order above, with a 300-second per-script timeout. Of 7 scripts: 0 ran without error, 5 errored, 2 timed out, 0 could not run because one of its own dependencies is unavailable, 0 did not parse. Running against current package versions: a break can reflect version drift, which argues for pinning versions. 1 script could not be tried, because a file it needs was not downloaded: 02_model_comparison_and_contrasts.R. It is not counted as a script that did not reproduce. Undefined-variable errors

1 script failed on an undefined variable or function (object '...' not found / could not find function "..."). This typically means the script expects a variable or function that another script defines — i.e. it is meant to be sourced into a larger session, not run on its own — or the symbol is simply never created. When another of the paper’s own script files defines the same name, this is called out below as a likely sourcing/copy-paste gap. Each is counted as an error (red).

Calls that install packages in the code

1 call to install.packages(), groundhog, checkpoint, renv, pacman and the like was replaced, since they install packages themselves: groundhog.library() and p_load() by library(), the others by a statement that does nothing. The scripts ran with the packages installed before the run.

Expand a row below to see the output each script produced.

> library(lme4)
> sim_power_glmm <- function(n, n_trials, effect_OR, slope_sd,
>                            n_groups = 3, n_sessions = 2, int_sd = 0.7, p0 = 0.5,
>                            nsim = 500, seed = 1){
>   set.seed(seed)
>   groups   <- if (n_groups == 3) c("Control", "Incoming", "Outgoing") else c("Incoming", "Outgoing")
>   sessions <- c("S1", "S2", "S3")[seq_len(n_sessions)]
>   base <- expand.grid(id = paste0("id", seq_len(n * n_groups)),
>                       Session = factor(sessions, levels = sessions),
>                       Condition = factor(c("C1", "C2"), levels = c("C1", "C2")))
>   ids <- levels(base$id)
>   base$Group  <- factor(rep(groups, each = n)[match(as.character(base$id), ids)], levels = groups)
>   base$sesnum <- as.integer(base$Session) - 1L
> 
>   # True interaction placed in one corner: reference group, last session, Condition C2
>   refG  <- as.integer(base$Group == groups[1])
>   Slast <- as.integer(base$Session == sessions[n_sessions])
>   C2    <- as.integer(base$Condition == "C2")
>   eta_fixed <- qlogis(p0) + log(effect_OR) * refG * Slast * C2
> 
>   # Per-participant random intercept + session slope + condition slope (independent)
>   Rt <- chol(diag(c(int_sd^2, slope_sd^2, slope_sd^2)))
> 
>   p <- numeric(nsim)
>   for (s in seq_len(nsim)) {
>     re <- matrix(rnorm(3 * length(ids)), ncol = 3) %*% Rt
>     rownames(re) <- ids
>     eta <- eta_fixed + re[as.character(base$id), 1] +
>            re[as.character(base$id), 2] * base$sesnum + re[as.character(base$id), 3] * C2
>     dat <- base
>     dat$succ <- rbinom(nrow(base), n_trials, plogis(eta)); dat$fail <- n_trials - dat$succ
> 
>     m1 <- try(suppressMessages(glmer(cbind(succ, fail) ~ Group * Session * Condition + (1 + sesnum + Condition | id),
>                                      family = binomial, data = dat,
>                                      control = glmerControl(optimizer = "bobyqa", calc.derivs = FALSE))), silent = TRUE)
>     if (inherits(m1, "try-error")) { p[s] <- NA; next }
>     m0 <- try(suppressMessages(update(m1, . ~ Group * Session + Group * Condition + Session * Condition +
>                                           (1 + sesnum + Condition | id))), silent = TRUE)
>     p[s] <- if (inherits(m0, "try-error")) NA else anova(m0, m1)$`Pr(>Chisq)`[2]
>   }
>   mean(p < .05, na.rm = TRUE)
> }
> required_n <- function(n_trials, effect_OR, slope_sd, n_groups, n_sessions,
>                        n_grid = c(15, 20, 25, 30, 40, 50, 60, 80, 100), target = 0.80, nsim = 500){
>   for (n in n_grid) {
>     pw <- sim_power_glmm(n, n_trials, effect_OR, slope_sd, n_groups = n_groups, n_sessions = n_sessions, nsim = nsim)
>     if (!is.na(pw) && pw >= target) return(data.frame(N_per_group = n, power = round(pw, 2)))
>   }
>   data.frame(N_per_group = NA, power = NA)
> }
> task_trials <- c(Bandit = 62, Simon = 40, Social = 64, SelfControl = 30)
> designs <- list("3groups_2sessions" = c(n_groups = 3, n_sessions = 2),
>                 "2groups_3sessions" = c(n_groups = 2, n_sessions = 3))
> EFFECT_OR <- 1.5
> SLOPE_SD <- 0.4
> NSIM <- 500
> results <- data.frame()
> for (task in names(task_trials)) for (dz in names(designs)) {
>   d <- designs[[dz]]
>   rn <- required_n(task_trials[[task]], EFFECT_OR, SLOPE_SD, d["n_groups"], d["n_sessions"], nsim = NSIM)
>   results <- rbind(results, data.frame(Task = task, Design = dz, n_trials = task_trials[[task]],
>                                        N_per_group = rn$N_per_group, power = rn$power))

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

Participants were recruited as part of a larger longitudinal project investigating the effects of incarceration on executive functioning and decision-making.

All participants provided written informed consent prior to participation.

Incoming and outgoing participants were recruited from 15 Belgian penitentiary institutions.

Incoming and outgoing participants are collectively referred to as prisoners.

Incoming participants were assessed within four to seven weeks following incarceration (M = 31.4 days, SD = 13.9).

Outgoing participants were assessed shortly before release (M = 19.7 days, SD = 35.0).

To minimize perceived links between the study and the judicial system, informed consent procedures were conducted without prison staff present and participants were explicitly informed that the research team operated independently from both prison authorities and the justice system.

All eligible candidates received oral and written information regarding the study and provided written informed consent.

Participants were reminded that they could withdraw from the study at any time without consequences.

Incoming and outgoing participants completed behavioural tasks with EEG recordings, and questionnaires and received financial compensation (€10 for questionnaires and €10 for experimental tasks).

Control participants completed the behavioural tasks and received course credits.

Control participants were significantly younger than both incoming and outgoing participants, and incoming participants were significantly younger than outgoing participants (all ps < .001).

Groups also differed in gender composition as community controls were predominantly female (34 female, 8 male), whereas incoming and outgoing participants were exclusively male.

Participants completed a probabilistic two-armed bandit task adapted from Aylward et al. (2019) programmed in PsychoPy (Python 3.10).

Participants were informed that their performance would determine a real monetary bonus constrained between €0 and €3.

In addition, because the control group comprised both women and men whereas all incarcerated participants were men, supplementary sensitivity analyses were conducted within the control group to evaluate potential effects of gender on behavioural, computational and neural outcomes.

Because participants were incarcerated 488 individuals, access to potentially identifying data may be subject to ethical and data-489 protection restrictions.

Participants were recruited across 15 penitentiary establishments in Belgium that varied in security level, detention regime, and capacity.

Consequently, substantially larger baseline samples were recruited to ensure adequate statistical power following expected attrition.

Incoming participants were primarily recruited from Brugge (32.2%), Nieuw Dendermonde (27.1%), and Jamioulx (18.6%), with smaller proportions from Tournai (8.5%), Marche-en-Famenne (6.8%), Turnhout (5.1%), and Huy (1.7%).

Outgoing participants were mainly recruited from Ittre (20.0%), Leuven Centraal (20.0%), and Leuze (16.0%), followed by Beveren (12.0%) and Saint-Hubert (12.0%).

Smaller proportions were recruited from Brugge, Hoogstraten, Jamioulx, Marche-en-Famenne, and Ruiselede (each 4.0%).

Percentage of participants recruited from each prison in the final S1-S2 complete sample, separately for incoming (IN) and outgoing (OUT) participants.

Participants were excluded if they had insufficient understanding of Dutch, French, or English, experienced visual or motor impairments, or exhibited inappropriate behaviour towards the researchers.

Participants were also excluded if they had already been approached during a previous incarceration period.

Incoming participants were additionally excluded if their incarceration was expected to be transient (e.g., imminent release, extradition, or deportation).

Outgoing participants were additionally required to have served at least six months in detention prior to the baseline assessment.

To facilitate longitudinal follow-up, participants provided contact details on paper-based forms.

Participants still in prison were contacted through institutional channels, whereas released participants were contacted using the information provided at baseline.

Incoming participants were notified of upcoming follow-up sessions through letters sent via the prison communication system (Prison Cloud) or via institutional intermediaries.

At approximately 3 months post-release, participants were contacted to schedule follow-up assessments.

Control participants were significantly younger than both incoming participants (mean difference = 15.91 years, p < .001, d = 1.82) and outgoing participants (mean difference = 23.83 years, p < .001, d = 2.73).

Incoming participants were also significantly younger than outgoing participants (mean difference = 7.92 years, p < .001, d = 0.91).

Education level was described only for the incoming and outgoing detainee groups because all control participants were university students.

Participants were retained only if they contributed at least 10 valid trials per outcome feedback and at least 30 feedback trials overall.

Because the control group comprised both women and men (34 F and 8 M) whereas all incarcerated participants were men, we could not include gender as a covariate in the full between-group models without confounding it with group.

Participants had up to 4 s to choose one option.

Ethics approval statement:

The study protocol was preregistered on the Open Science Framework (OSF; DOI: https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/596QD) and approved by the Ethics Committee of Ghent University (2023-085A).

Preregistration Check

id title template
596qd Effect of Imprisonment on decision-making under reward and punishment conditions: A Longitudinal EEG Study of newly detained prisoners. OSF Preregistration

Meta-scientific research has shown that deviations from preregistrations are often not reported or checked, and that the most common deviations concern the sample size. We recommend manually checking the full preregistration at the link above, and have provided the preregistered sample size.

id sample_size
596qd The final target sample size for this study across all groups (control, incoming and outgoing) is approximatively 30 total participants completing the full study. Considering the high rates of attrition in studies involving prisoners with long periods of follow-up (Dirkzwager & Nieuwbeerta, 2018; Gonçalves et al., 2016; Hassan et al., 2011; Lennox et al., 2013), we seek to recruit 300 incoming and 300 outgoing participants for the baseline assessment. For the control group we only seek to recruit 60 participants at baseline.
Initial sample size estimates were based on a priori G*Power calculations conducted for repeated-measures designs, using a medium effect size (f = .25) and 95% power. These analyses provided approximate minimum sample-size targets for longitudinal group comparisons, but they were not based on the final Bayesian reinforcement-learning parameter analyses.
For the detainee groups, an initial repeated-measures framework with two groups and three sessions suggested that approximately 28 participants would be required. For analyses including the control group over the shared two-session interval, an additional estimate suggested a required sample of approximately 45 participants. These estimates were used as feasibility benchmarks rather than definitive power estimates for the final computational modeling analyses.
Field Preregistration 1
template_name OSF Preregistration
title Effect of Imprisonment on decision-making under reward and punishment conditions: A Longitudinal EEG Study of newly detained prisoners.
id 596qd
link https://osf.io/596qd
date_created 2023-08-08T16:09:08.537370
date_modified 2026-05-31T13:28:22.802681
date_registered 2023-08-08T16:09:08.516999
embargo_end_date
ia_url https://archive.org/details/osf-registrations-596qd-v1
research_questions • A recent exploratory study suggested that imprisonment may lead to increased risk-taking after three months (Meijers et al., 2018). However, there is currently no longitudinal evidence on how decision-making processes evolve during incarceration and following release. Therefore, we adopt a bidirectional hypothesis framework.
H1: At the behavioural level, we hypothesize that imprisonment and release may alter decision-making processes under uncertainty, as measured in the bandit task. The primary outcomes of interest will be model-derived parameters capturing core aspects of decision-making. According to the model of best-fit, these may include learning dynamics, sensitivity to reward and punishment, as well as other parameters reflecting choice stochasticity or decision noise. We hypothesize that these latent parameters may either increase or decrease over time across the 8-month follow-up period.
H1.a: During incarceration, incoming prisoners may show longitudinal changes in reinforcement learning parameters. Specifically, they may exhibit a reduction or an increase in learning rates; which may be accompanied by altered sensitivities to reward and punishment. Depending on the direction of these changes, we hypothesize that they will reflect (1) higher impulsivity or risk-taking behaviour, or (2) behavioural stabilization or adaptation to the structured prison environment. Changes may also be observed in parameters capturing noise, indicating shifts in decision consistency.
H1.b: Following release, ex-prisoners may also show bidirectional changes in reinforcement learning parameters. Learning rates and reward/punishment sensitivities may increase, reflecting adaptation to a more dynamic and uncertain environment, or decrease due to stress and instability associated with reintegration. Similarly, parameters indexing choice stochasticity may either decrease, indicating more consistent decision-making, or increase, reflecting greater variability in behaviour.

• At the neural level, feedback processing will be assessed using EEG markers associated with reward and punishment learning. Punishment processing is indexed by frontal midline theta band power and the feedback-related negativity (FRN), which reflect punishment prediction errors and scale with outcome surprisal (Cavanagh et al., 2010; Chase et al., 2011; Holroyd & Coles, 2002; Williams et al., 2021). Reward processing is indexed by increased delta band power and the reward positivity (Rew-P), which reflect reward prediction errors and are modulated by outcome probability (Holroyd et al., 2008; Sambrook & Goslin, 2014; Walsh & Anderson, 2012).
H2: Given the absence of prior longitudinal research in incarcerated populations, we propose bidirectional hypotheses regarding neural sensitivity to feedback. H2.a : During incarceration, neural responses to reward (delta band power and Rew-P amplitude) and punishment (theta band power and FRN amplitude) may either increase or decrease over time.
H2.b : Similarly, after release, these neural markers may show either recovery, further attenuation, or continued alteration across the 8-month follow-up period.

• H3 : Furthermore, individual differences in personality traits related to the Behavioral Inhibition System and Behavioral Activation System are expected to moderate these effects. Specifically, some individuals may show increased punishment sensitivity over time, while others may show a decrease, with similar variability expected for reward sensitivity
• H4 : Finally, for the outgoing group, the magnitude and direction of post-release changes may be modulated by the duration of prior incarceration, with longer detention potentially leading to more persistent or attenuated effects on decision-making and feedback processing.
study_design_overview The study is a repeated-measures design with time as a within-subject factor (Baseline, 4 months, 8 months). Participants include both incoming detainees assessed shortly after incarceration and outgoing detainees assessed prior to release, recruited from French and Flemish prisons in Belgium. In addition, a community control group is included to account for repeated task exposure effects, with one 4-month interval re-test only. Incoming participants are recruited within the first three to seven weeks following incarceration, whereas outgoing participants are assessed approximately one month prior to release. All incoming and outgoing participants are followed longitudinally at 4- and 8-month intervals for a total of 3 sessions.
Participants will complete a two-armed bandit task lasting approximately 15 minutes. In this task, participants repeatedly choose between two options (bandits) associated with probabilistic outcomes. The reward contingencies are not explicitly known and must be learned through trial-by-trial feedback. The task is designed to assess decision-making under uncertainty, specifically how participants learn from reward and punishment and how they balance exploration of uncertain options with exploitation of known rewarding options.
Behavioral measures include trial-by-trial choices and reaction times. In addition to descriptive performance metrics (e.g., proportion of optimal choices), computational modeling of behavior will be conducted using reinforcement learning models. Model-derived parameters will be extracted from the model of best-fit, to quantify latent cognitive processes, including learning dynamics, sensitivity to reward and punishment, and decision variability (e.g., stochasticity or lapse processes). Neural activity will be recorded throughout the task using electroencephalography (EEG).
Importantly, repeated task exposure may induce practice effects, such as improved performance, faster reaction times, and more stable model-derived parameters over time, independent of incarceration-related changes. The inclusion of a control group assessed at baseline and one matched interval (i.e. 4-month retest only) allows these effects to be quantified and controlled. Accordingly, longitudinal changes observed in incoming and outgoing participants will be interpreted relative to changes in the control group, enabling dissociation between practice-related effects and incarceration-related cognitive changes.
randomization The reward structure followed a nonstationary two-armed bandit paradigm in which outcome probabilities varied dynamically over time and were periodically reversed between bandits, such that the option with the higher expected value changed across blocks. This structure was identical across participants.
In addition, several task features were randomized to prevent systematic biases. Specifically, the exact trial-by-trial reward probabilities were generated stochastically for each participant, resulting in unique probabilistic sequences. Furthermore, the color assignment of the bandits was randomized between participants and sessions. And their spatial position (left versus right) were randomized across trials. This ensured that learning was not driven by perceptual or spatial preferences. In addition, this randomization minimized the risk of participants sharing task-related information and attempting to anticipate the advantageous option across sessions, which would not generalize due to the stochastic and nonstationary nature of the reward probabilities.
data_collection_procedures As part of this study, incoming detainees, outgoing detainees, and community control participants will be recruited. Incoming participants will consist of newly detained prisoners, selected from weekly lists of individuals newly received into custody in French and Flemish prisons in Belgium. Outgoing participants will consist of prisoners approaching release. During recruitment periods, we will aim to approach all eligible detainees and invite them to participate, with recruitment timing adapted to institutional and logistical constraints.
Community control participants will consist of first-year psychology students from Ghent University, recruited via the SONA system. Control participants will complete the same task at baseline and 4-month retest.
Prisoners will be excluded if they were transferred from other establishments, are scheduled for deportation or awaiting extradition (incoming group), or if they have insufficient understanding of Dutch or French, present visual or motor impairments, or exhibit inappropriate behavior toward the researcher.
Baseline assessments (T1) will take place in prison, three to seven weeks after incarceration for incoming participants and prior to release for outgoing participants. Follow-up testing sessions will be conducted at 4 (T2) and 8 months (T3) after baseline assessment. Control participants will be assessed at the first 2 sessions only (4-month re-test).
At each assessment, comprehensive contact information will be collected, reviewed, and updated for detainees, including residential address, mobile phone number, email address, and, when available, social media contact details. Participants will also be asked to provide contact information for alternative contacts (e.g., family members or friends), and to provide consent for these contacts to be used in case follow-up is required and their personal contact information has changed.
Participants will not be excluded based on the presence of mental disorders at recruitment. However, if participants are unable to complete the task or are non-compliant, they may be excluded from the analyses.
Participants will receive compensation for their participation. Incoming detainees will be compensated via their prison bank account. Outgoing participants will be compensated via bank transfer. Control participants will receive course credits for their participation at each timepoint.
sample_size The final target sample size for this study across all groups (control, incoming and outgoing) is approximatively 30 total participants completing the full study. Considering the high rates of attrition in studies involving prisoners with long periods of follow-up (Dirkzwager & Nieuwbeerta, 2018; Gonçalves et al., 2016; Hassan et al., 2011; Lennox et al., 2013), we seek to recruit 300 incoming and 300 outgoing participants for the baseline assessment. For the control group we only seek to recruit 60 participants at baseline.
Initial sample size estimates were based on a priori G*Power calculations conducted for repeated-measures designs, using a medium effect size (f = .25) and 95% power. These analyses provided approximate minimum sample-size targets for longitudinal group comparisons, but they were not based on the final Bayesian reinforcement-learning parameter analyses.
For the detainee groups, an initial repeated-measures framework with two groups and three sessions suggested that approximately 28 participants would be required. For analyses including the control group over the shared two-session interval, an additional estimate suggested a required sample of approximately 45 participants. These estimates were used as feasibility benchmarks rather than definitive power estimates for the final computational modeling analyses.
sample_size_rationale Initial sample size estimates were based on a priori G*Power calculations (Faul et al., 2007), conducted for repeated-measures designs, using a medium effect size (f = .25) and 95% power. These analyses provided approximate minimum sample-size targets for longitudinal group comparisons, but they were not based on the final Bayesian reinforcement-learning parameter analyses.
First, a power analysis was conducted for the incarcerated groups only, including incoming and outgoing detainees. This analysis used a mixed repeated-measures framework with 2 groups and 6 repeated measurements, reflecting the combination of the 3-level session factor and the 2-level feedback (i.e. win or lose) factor. For detecting a medium effect size (f = .25), with 95% power, the required total sample size was estimated at 28 participants (i.e. 14 per group).
Second, an additional power analysis included the community control group for additional analyses of practice effects. Because control participants were assessed at two sessions, this analysis used a mixed repeated-measures framework with 3 groups and 4 repeated measurements, corresponding to the 2-level session factor and the 2-level feedback factor. Under the same assumptions, the required total sample size was estimated at 45 participants (i.e. 15 per group).
However, this project is part of a larger study for which, keeping all other factors constant, an additional power analysis was conducted focusing on within-subject effects across 3 sessions, with 2 conditions (i.e. each task of the study includes at least 2 conditions), and was performed separately within each detainee group. This analysis yielded a required sample size of 28 participants per group.
The final behavioral analyses rely on hierarchical Bayesian reinforcement-learning models and posterior highest density interval contrasts of model-derived parameters. Because formal frequentist power calculations do not directly map onto this inferential framework, the original power analyses are reported only as approximate recruitment targets. The substantially larger baseline recruitment target was chosen to compensate for expected attrition and to maximize the number of participants contributing usable longitudinal data.
measured_variables • The primary behavioral outcome variables will be determined by computational modeling of behavior using reinforcement learning models. Model-derived parameters will be extracted to capture latent cognitive processes, including learning rates and sensitivity to reward and punishment, as well as parameters indexing choice variability (e.g., stochasticity or lapse processes), if they are identified by the model of best-fit. These parameters will serve as key outcome variables for assessing changes in decision-making over time.
• At the neural level, feedback-locked event-related potentials will be examined, focusing on the feedback-related negativity (FRN) and reward positivity (Rew-P). These components will be quantified following feedback onset and compared between outcome types (win versus loss) and across sessions.
• In addition, time-frequency analyses will be conducted to assess theta-band and delta-band power in response to feedback. Theta activity is expected to reflect loss-related processing, whereas delta activity is commonly associated with reward/positive feedback processing. Analyses will compare power as a function of outcome (win versus loss) and across sessions.
• Reaction time (RT) will be included as a covariate in the analyses, as an index of decision processes (e.g., impulsivity or deliberation), rather than as a primary outcome variable.
• Demographic variables will be collected, including age, gender, level of education, nature of the offense(s) leading to incarceration, length of incarceration, past criminal records, and socio-economic status.
• Personality and mental health measures will be included by means of self-reported questionnaires. Participants will rate each item based on its applicability over the preceding four weeks.
• For incarcerated participants, perception of the prison environment will be assessed at baseline using the Prison Climate Questionnaire (PCQ; Bosma et al., 2020), a validated instrument measuring perceived prison conditions and social relationships within the institution.
• Following release (from T2 onwards), quality of life will be assessed using the Good Lives Questionnaire (GLQ; Harper et al., 2021), which is adapted to measure well-being and life conditions outside the prison environment.
indices The primary outcome variables are model-derived parameters obtained from computational reinforcement learning models. In particular, parameters will be extracted from the model of best-fit. These parameters could include :
(i) learning dynamics (e.g., learning rates),
(ii) sensitivity to reward and punishment outcomes, and
(iii) decision processes, including choice stochasticity or exploration.
statistical_tests Behavioral data will first be analyzed using hierarchical Bayesian reinforcement-learning models adapted from previous work applying computational modeling to probabilistic bandit tasks (Ahn et al., 2017; Aylward et al., 2019). Primary behavioral outcomes will consist of model-derived parameters obtained from computational reinforcement learning models. These parameters will be estimated using hierarchical Bayesian reinforcement-learning models fit separately for each group-session dataset, yielding posterior estimates of both group-level and individual-level latent decision-making parameters.
Group and session differences in these parameters will be assessed using Bayesian posterior contrasts. Specifically, highest density interval (HDI) contrasts will be computed to compare posterior distributions between groups (incoming, outgoing, controls) at each session, as well as within-group longitudinal changes across sessions. This approach allows direct probabilistic inference on parameter differences without relying on null-hypothesis significance testing. Practice effects will be evaluated by comparing longitudinal changes in the control group to those observed in incarcerated and ex-incarcerated groups.
Neural analyses will focus on feedback-locked components associated with reward and punishment processing, including event-related potentials (ERPs), namely the reward positivity (Rew-P) and feedback-related negativity (FRN) amplitudes, as well as delta and theta bands power. Neural outcomes will be analyzed using linear mixed-effects models. Fixed effects will include Group, Session, Feedback (win versus loss), and their interactions. Participant will be included as a random effect, with random intercepts and, where appropriate, random slopes for within-subject factors to capture individual variability.
When significant effects are observed in the neural models, post hoc pairwise comparisons will be conducted using estimated marginal means, with appropriate correction for multiple comparisons. Model assumptions will be assessed through standard diagnostic procedures, including inspection of residual distributions and evaluation of model fit.
transformations Raw EEG data will be preprocessed. We will use a Biosemi 32-electrode EEG system with four external electrodes. Two of the external electrodes will be placed on the left and right mastoids. Data will be acquired using ActiveView software, and subsequently converted to BIDS format. To filter and clean the data, we will use MNE-Python (Gramfort et al., 2013). The data will be re-referenced using the reference electrode standardization technique (REST). Different band-pass filters will be applied for event-related potentials (ERPs) and for time-frequency analyses. For stimulus-locked activity, the baseline will be taken before the stimulus presentation. Since participants will not be placed in a Faraday Cage, a Notch filter will be applied to remove the power line noise at 50 Hz. To remove ocular and muscular artifacts, Independent Component Analysis (ICA). Artifact-related components will be identified using a combination of automatic classification and visual inspection of component topographies and time courses, and subsequently removed.
inference_criteria Behavioural reinforcement-learning parameters will be compared using Bayesian posterior contrasts of group-level parameter distributions. Differences will be interpreted using highest density intervals. Neural outcomes will be analyzed using linear mixed-effects models. For these neural models, statistical significance will be evaluated using a two-sided α level of .05, and post hoc estimated marginal mean comparisons will be corrected for multiple comparisons where applicable.
data_exclusion_criteria Outliers will be identified at the trial level using a within-participant 2-of-3 rule, combining three criteria: median absolute deviation (MAD > 3), z-score (|z| > 3), and interquartile range (IQR beyond 1.5 × IQR). Trials flagged by at least two of these criteria will be excluded from subsequent analyses.
EEG data will be excluded when excessive artifacts are present. Artifact rejection will be based on a combination of automated detection procedures and visual inspection. Trials contaminated by noise (e.g., due to eye movements, muscle activity, or signal instability) will be removed. Participants contributing an insufficient number of valid trials after preprocessing will be excluded from EEG analyses.
exploratory_analyses Scores on questionnaires assessing mental health, personality, and quality of life inside and outside prison, assessed using the PCQ and GLQ respectively, will be examined as potential moderators of longitudinal changes in reward and punishment processing. Specifically, these measures will be included as covariates in exploratory analyses to test whether individual differences in prison climate perception, post-release quality of life, and psychological functioning are associated with variability in behavioral parameters and neural markers.
The BIS/BAS scale assesses individual sensitivity to reward and punishment, as well as broader motivational tendencies (Carver & White, 1994). BIS/BAS scores will be used to examine whether trait-level motivational sensitivity moderates longitudinal changes in reinforcement-learning parameters and neural indices of reward and punishment processing. In particular, we will test whether individual differences in behavioral inhibition and behavioral activation predict differential trajectories of reward sensitivity, punishment sensitivity, and associated feedback-related neural responses over the 8-month follow-up period. Although BIS/BAS is generally conceptualized as reflecting relatively stable trait characteristics, exploratory analyses will also assess whether these scores show systematic change across the follow-up period.
study_type Experiment - A researcher randomly assigns treatments to study subjects, this includes field or lab experiments. This is also known as an intervention experiment and includes randomized controlled trials.
blinding No blinding is involved in this study.
existing_data Registration prior to creation of data
manipulated_variables We will manipulate the probability of winning for each bandit.
missing_data_handling If data are missing on questionnaires, we will replace the missing data by using a multivariate imputation by chained equations algorithm (MICE) which models each feature with missing values as a function of all the other variables, and uses that prediction estimate for imputation (Pedregosa et al., 2011).
additional_comments This study is part of a larger project investigating the changes in executive functions induced by imprisonment and the differential impact of prison regimes. The protocol aims at answering several additional research questions, regarding emotion recognition ability, cognitive control, sensitivity to reward and punishment and anti-social behaviour.
paper_id input

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate of deviations from preregistrations, see:

van den Akker O, Bakker M, van Assen M, Pennington C, Verweij L, Elsherif M, Claesen A, Gaillard S, Yeung S, Frankenberger J, Krautter K, Cockcroft J, Kreuer K, Evans T, Heppel F, Schoch S, Korbmacher M, Yamada Y, Albayrak-Aydemir N, Wicherts J (2024). “The potential of preregistration in psychology: Assessing preregistration producibility and preregistration-study consistency.” Psychological Methods. doi:10.1037/met0000687.

For educational material on how to report deviations from preregistrations, see:

Lakens, Daniël (2024). “When and How to Deviate From a Preregistration.” Collabra: Psychology, 10(1), 117094. doi:10.1525/collabra.117094.

Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests

All detected t-tests and F-tests had an effect size reported in the same sentence.

For F-tests with a reported eta-squared effect size, coherence checks yielded 1 indeterminate case.
