How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Levi Baruch

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Imprisonment intends to modify future behaviour partly through punishment. However, little is known about how incarceration alters reinforcement-learning and decision-making. This preregistered longitudinal study investigated behavioural and neural changes in reward and punishment processing during incarceration and release. Newly-incarcerated prisoners (Incoming; n = 61), newly-released individuals (Outgoing; n = 25), and community controls (n = 42) completed a probabilistic reinforcement-learning task at baseline and four months later.

Hierarchical Bayesian computational modelling estimated reward and punishment sensitivities, learning rates, and lapse responding. EEG measured feedback-related negativity (FRN), feedback-locked frontal midline theta (FMθ), and delta activity during outcome evaluation. Compared to controls, incoming prisoners exhibited reduced reward sensitivity and learning rate at follow-up, whereas both incoming and outgoing participants showed greater punishment learning. Only controls showed the expected reduction in punishment learning rate across sessions. Behaviourally, controls made more optimal choices than prisoners at baseline, although outgoing participants improved to the same level as controls after release. Neurally, loss-win FRN differentiation strengthened across sessions in all groups. Delta activity was enhanced in both prisoner groups following reward and punishment, whereas FMθ was elevated overall in incoming prisoners. Computational and neurophysiological findings jointly indicate that incarceration is associated with alterations in reinforcement learning, characterized by reduced reward learning, sustained reliance on punishment, and altered neural feedback processing. This shift toward vigilance for negative outcomes may be adaptive within the restrictive prison environment, yet a reliance on punishment information also present after release could shape decision-making during community re-entry and could undermine successful reintegration.

Introduction

According to reinforcement learning (RL) theories, humans learn by updating their expectations from the rewards and punishments that follow their actions (Rescorla & Wagner, 1972; Sutton & Barto, 1998). These principles extend beyond everyday decisionmaking and are embedded in societal systems, including judicial institutions explicitly designed to influence behaviour through consequences. Imprisonment is partly justified by deterrence, that is, experiencing punishment reduces future offending (Nagin, 2013). Despite this rationale, recidivism rates remain substantial across many countries. A recent global systematic review reported that 18% to 55% of released prisoners are reconvicted within two years (Yukhnenko et al., 2023). These figures fuel ongoing debates regarding the extent to which imprisonment achieves its intended goals and highlight the importance of understanding the neural and cognitive mechanisms through which incarceration may influence future behaviour. In particular, little is known about how reward and punishment processing changes during incarceration and following release. Through computational modelling (Daw et al., 2011; Huys et al., 2016), previous research has shown that individual differences in reward and punishment sensitivity can determine how strongly rewards and punishments influence learning and decision-making. Importantly, these parameters appear sensitive to both psychopathology and environmental context. For example, depression and anxiety disorders have been associated with altered learning from positive and negative outcomes (Pulcu & Browning, 2019; Robinson & Chase, 2017), whereas substance use disorders have been linked to abnormalities in reward valuation, punishment responsiveness, and learning (Goldstein & Volkow, 2011; Huys et al., 2016). Environmental experiences such as acute stress and exposure to childhood adversity have been associated with such alterations too (Hanson et al., 2017; Kasparek et al., 2023; Lighthall et al., 2012; Nussenbaum & Hartley, 2024). As an environment characterized by stress, incarceration represents an important environmental change. Incarceration involves institutional control, social separation, limited access to rewarding experiences, and exposure to a range of chronic stressors (Liebling & Maruna, 2005; Sykes, 1958). These conditions may influence the subjective valuation of rewards and punishments and, consequently, the way individuals learn from them. Evidence on the effects of incarceration itself remains scarce and has primarily focused on broader constructs such as self-control and risk-taking (Aharoni et al., 2013; Meijers et al., 2018; Mitchell & MacKenzie, 2006). Atypical reward and punishment processing has been reported at the behavioural and neural levels in offender populations (Dillien et al., 2020; Gregory et al., 2015; Murray et al., 2018; Newman & Kosson, 1986).

However, these studies conceptualize these alterations in reward and punishment processing as stable characteristics associated with offending. Consequently, it remains unclear whether observed differences in RL and feedback processing reflect the effects of incarceration or preexisting individual characteristics. To our knowledge, no longitudinal studies have directly examined, at the behavioural and neural level, whether reward and punishment sensitivities change during incarceration or following release from prison.

At the neural level, the feedback-related negativity (FRN) and frontal midline theta (FMθ) activity have consistently been associated with negative outcomes processing, and reward prediction errors (Cavanagh et al., 2010; Holroyd & Coles, 2002; Williams et al., 2021). These signals are thought to reflect neural processes involved in evaluating outcomes and updating behaviour following worse-than-expected events (Cavanagh et al., 2010; Holroyd & Coles, 2002). In contrast, delta-band activity has been linked to positive outcome processing and reward valuation (Cavanagh, 2015; Glazer et al., 2018). Longitudinal changes in these neural markers have not yet been examined in incarcerated populations and can help identify the neurocognitive mechanisms through which incarceration alters RL, by revealing changes in outcome evaluation and prediction error signalling that are not captured by behavioural measures alone.

The present preregistered longitudinal study investigated how incarceration and release influence reward and punishment processing at both behavioural and neural levels. Newly incarcerated prisoners ("incoming" group), newly released individuals ("outgoing" group), and community controls completed a probabilistic RL bandit task (Aylward et al., 2019), at two time points separated by approximately four months. Computational modelling was used to estimate latent parameters reflecting reward and punishment sensitivity, learning, and value-independent responding. EEG was recorded during feedback processing, with analyses focusing on the feedback-related negativity (FRN) and oscillatory activity in the theta and delta bands. The study addressed how reward and punishment processing changes over time during incarceration and following release from prison. It also aims to distinguish these environment-dependent changes from ordinary practice effects, using a repeated-testing control group. The prison environment may alter sensitivity to rewards because of restricted access to rewarding experiences. Likewise, it can lead to increased sensitivity to punishments through repeated exposure to aversive conditions, or, alternatively, lead to adaptation and reduced responsiveness to negative outcomes. Similarly, reintegration into society following release may again be accompanied by further changes in reward and punishment processing.

Given the absence of prior longitudinal research on RL in incarcerated populations, and the limited and mixed cross-sectional evidence available, bidirectional hypotheses were preregistered.

Results

Behavioural Performance

Behavioural performance was indexed by the probability of selecting the optimal bandit (P(optimal choice)). Omnibus tests (Type-III Wald χ², effects coding) revealed significant main effects of Group (χ²(2) = 18.40, p < .001) and Block (χ²(4) = 23.78, p < .001), a marginal effect of Session (χ²(1) = 3.81, p = .051), and no effect of Age (χ²(1) = 2.85, p = .091). These were qualified by a significant Group × Session × Block interaction (χ²(8) = 16.70, p = .033; Fig. 3), whereas the Group × Session (p = .599), Group × Block (p = .102), and Session × Block (p = .140) interactions were not significant. Planned contrasts further clarified the significant three-way interaction. At S1, control participants selected the optimal option more frequently than both incoming (OR = 1.34, p < .001) and outgoing prisoners (OR = 1.48, p = .011), whereas incoming and outgoing participants did not differ (p = .700). At S2, controls continued to outperform incoming participants (OR = 1.25, p = .022). Incoming and outgoing participants did not differ at S2 (p = .931). Within-group longitudinal contrasts revealed no significant changes in optimal choice performance from S1 to S2 in any group (all ps ≥ .088). All results are included in Appendix 9. Session × Block interaction where controls selected the optimal option more frequently than both prisoner groups, most markedly at baseline (S1), with the group differences narrowing at follow-up (S2).

Computational modelling

The winning model (banditNarm_lapse) estimated separate reward and punishment learning rates and sensitivities together with a lapse parameter capturing value-independent responding (Fig. 4; all posterior contrasts in Tables S5-4 and S5-5). At baseline (S1), the three groups did not differ credibly on any parameter. However, at follow-up (S2), incoming participants exhibited lower reward sensitivity than Controls (95% HDI [-2.582, -0.420]), lower reward learning rates (95% HDI [-0.390, -0.011]), and higher punishment learning rates (95% HDI [0.050, 0.470]). Outgoing participants also showed higher punishment learning rates than Controls at S2 (95% HDI [0.171, 0.592]). Incoming and Outgoing participants did not differ credibly at either session.

Longitudinal analyses revealed that controls showed a credible decrease in punishment learning rate from S1 to S2 (95% HDI [-0.43, -0.02]), whereas prisoners did not (Incoming ≈ 0; Outgoing +0.10, 95% HDI [-0.22, 0.43]). The corresponding Group × Session contrasts were suggestive but not credible (Incoming -Control pd = .93; Outgoing -Control pd = .95).

Therefore, the decrease is most evident in controls but it is not evidence of a reliable group difference in the longitudinal change. Lapse responding decreased from S1 to S2 in all groups, reaching credibility only in the (largest) incoming group (95% HDI [-0.085, -0.004]). The same directional decrease was present in controls and outgoing participants, and none of the Group × Session contrasts were credible (all 95% HDIs included zero), most likely reflecting a common reduction in random responding with repeated testing. No other parameter changed credibly. Because the punishment learning rate and lapse were weakly identified at the individual level and strongly shrunk toward the group mean (Appendix 5), individual differences in these two parameters should be interpreted with caution. S5-4 and S5-5.

EEG Responses to Feedback

Three feedback-related neural measures were examined. The FRN and feedback-locked FMθ both index the processing of negative (loss) feedback, whereas delta power reflects the motivational salience of outcomes. First, the way each group differentiated losses from wins (loss-win contrasts) was examined, followed by how this differentiation changed across sessions, and finally overall group differences in outcome reactivity (delta).

Differentiation of Losses From Wins (FRN and FMθ Power)

For FRN amplitude, a significant main effect of Outcome, F(1, 85.30) = 15.20, p < .001, was qualified by a significant Session × Outcome interaction, F(1, 1743.39) = 13.37, p < .001.

Planned contrasts showed that loss-win differentiation strengthened from S1 to S2, driven by loss trials (win-related FRN was stable in every group, all ps ≥ .179). This loss-related strengthening was significant in incoming participants (Lose S2-S1 = -0.471, p = .035), a nonsignificant trend in outgoing participants (-0.655, p = .065), and absent in controls (-0.070, p = .791). However, the Group × Session × Outcome interaction was not significant, F(2, 1743.39) = 1.33, p = .264 (nor Group × Outcome, F(2, 100.64) = 1.26, p = .289, nor the main effect of Group, F(2, 101.97) = 1.75, p = .179), so although the strengthening was descriptively confined to the prisoner groups, a group-specific difference was not statistically established.

By follow-up, all three groups showed reliable loss-win differentiation (Table 1; Fig. 5). Age was negatively associated with FRN amplitude (β = -0.038, p = .011). FMθ power showed a significant main effect of Group, F(2, 114.3) = 3.31, p = .040, with incoming prisoners showing higher overall FMθ power than controls (Fig. 5). No other main effect or interaction was significant (all ps ≥ .103), so the group difference did not reliably vary across sessions or feedback types.

Group Differences in Outcome Reactivity (delta)

Delta power showed a significant main effect of Group, F(2, 114.3) = 5.17, p = .007, with no effect of Session, Outcome, or any interaction (all ps ≥ .16). Both prisoner groups exhibited greater delta power than controls across sessions and outcomes (incoming vs. controls, all p ≤ .033; outgoing vs. controls, all p ≤ .051), whereas incoming and outgoing prisoners did not differ (all ps ≥ .345; Table 2; Fig. 5). A significant main effect of Age was also observed, F(1, 108.6) = 4.31, p = .040, with older participants showing lower delta power (β = -0.015).

Importantly, this group difference was not attributable to age since age and group were associated with delta in opposite directions. Indeed, older participants showed lower delta 195 power, yet the prisoner groups, although substantially older than controls, showed higher 196 delta power. 197

Discussion

The present study investigated longitudinal changes in RL and feedback processing during incarceration and following release using computational modelling and EEG measures. Three main findings emerged. First, behavioural performance and computational modelling provided converging evidence for altered RL processes in prisoners relative to controls, characterized by reduced reward-based learning and sustained punishment-based learning. Second, both prisoner groups showed globally enhanced delta power to outcomes, indicating altered neural evaluation of feedback. Third, loss-win FRN differentiation strengthened across repeated testing in prisoners, suggesting an incarceration-related change.

Contrary to our hypothesis, RL parameters showed little longitudinal change. Lapse responding decreased in all three groups from S1 to S2. Although this longitudinal change only reached credibility in the larger incoming group, it did not differ credibly between groups, suggesting a common decrease in random responding and greater choice consistency with repeated testing rather than an incarceration-specific change. The most notable findings emerged from comparisons with controls. At baseline, controls performed better than both incoming and outgoing participants on the task, and again better than incoming participants at S2. This behavioural pattern coincided with lower reward sensitivity and learning rates in incoming participants at S2, suggesting that poorer task performance may reflect reduced use of rewarding outcomes to guide future choices rather than a general impairment in learning.

Moreover, both incoming and outgoing participants exhibited higher punishment learning rates than controls at follow-up. Together, these findings indicate a relative shift towards punishment-driven learning during incarceration. This pattern is consistent with theories suggesting that stressful environments heighten sensitivity to potential threats and losses, thereby strengthening punishment-based learning while reducing responsiveness to rewards (Browning et al., 2015; Carver & White, 1994; Robinson et al., 2013). Importantly, these effects emerged despite the absence of credible baseline differences in the computational parameters between groups. These group differences, present at follow-up but not at baseline, are more consistent with an adaptation developing during incarceration than with a stable, pre-existing characteristic. However, the results do not indicate a statistically established longitudinal change and the modest longitudinal sample means it cannot be firmly separated from a difference that was simply undetectable at baseline.

The inclusion of a longitudinal control group was essential for distinguishing incarcerationrelated changes from task repetition effects. Consistent with recent evidence that negative (punishment) learning rates decline with repeated testing (Xu et al., 2025), controls showed a reduced punishment learning rate over repeated task exposure. Repeated experience may encourage greater reliance on accumulated value representations, making isolated negative outcomes progressively less informative (Chierchia et al., 2023; Lefebvre et al., 2022; Palminteri et al., 2017). In contrast, prisoners did not show the practice-related reduction in punishment learning rate observed in controls. One possible explanation is that adaptation to prison requires sustained vigilance to negative events and behavioural consequences.

Within such environments, punishment-related information may remain highly salient, reducing the typical practice-related tendency to discount negative feedback (Haney, 2012; Zamble & Porporino, 1988). However, this divergence should be interpreted cautiously.

Although punishment learning rate decreased credibly only in controls, the Group × Session contrasts did not reach credibility, so the difference is suggestive rather than established, a limitation associated with the modest longitudinal sample.

At the neural level, FMθ was elevated overall in incoming prisoners relative to controls, consistent with heightened feedback monitoring during adaptation to an unfamiliar and uncertain environment (Cavanagh & Frank, 2014; Cavanagh et al., 2010). In contrast, loss-win FRN differentiation strengthened over repeated testing. Loss-related FRN became more negative from S1 to S2, with the loss-related FRN increase reaching significance only in incoming participants, such that all three groups showed reliable differentiation by follow-up.

Larger negative FRN responses are generally interpreted as reflecting enhanced processing of outcomes that are worse than expected (Holroyd & Coles, 2002; Sambrook & Goslin, 2015).

Because this longitudinal strengthening was only significant in incoming prisoners and a trend in outgoing prisoners, but not present in controls, we cannot attribute it to pratice-related effects. In addition, it runs counter to the more common observation that feedback-related ERPs attenuate as outcomes become predictable through learning (Bellebaum & Daum, 2008), consistent with the RL account of the FRN (Holroyd & Coles, 2002). Because the present task involved reversing contingencies, however, outcomes never became fully predictable.

Thus, one possibility is that at re-test participants brought sharper task expectations, so that unexpected losses generated larger PE and a more negative loss FRN (Hajcak et al., 2007).

However, because the Group × Session × Outcome interaction did not reach significance, the difference between prisoners and controls was not statistically established, possibly reflecting limited power in the small outgoing sample. This is best read as a suggestive, candidate incarceration-related change to be confirmed in adequately powered designs. The shift toward punishment processing observed in incarcerated populations is therefore carried by the computational and delta findings rather than by the FRN. Both incoming and outgoing participants exhibited greater delta power than controls across outcomes and sessions. Delta oscillations have consistently been associated with motivational salience, reward evaluation, and RL processes (Cavanagh, 2015; Glazer et al., 2018; Watts & Bernat, 2018). In addition, older age was associated with lower delta amplitudes, consistent with age-related reductions in reward responsiveness and motivational processing (Eppinger et al., 2011; Samanez-Larkin & Knutson, 2015). Nonetheless, the opposite directions of the age and group associations make an age-driven account of the delta difference unlikely. In addition, delta differences were present following both reward and punishment feedback, hence they likely reflect altered processing of outcome salience rather than reward processing specifically (Mei et al., 2018). Although enhanced delta activity was present in both incoming and outgoing participants, only incoming participants exhibited reduced reward sensitivity and reward learning, whereas altered punishment learning was observed in both groups. This suggests that enhanced neural responses during outcome evaluation are not necessarily associated with more effective behavioural updating, but may instead reflect alterations in how feedback information is processed and incorporated into subsequent decisions (Ernst & Steinhauser, 2012; Xiang et al., 2026). Prior work in offender populations has similarly reported atypical, though heterogeneous, feedback-based decision-making, including altered gain/loss valuation (Pachur et al., 2010), impaired reward and punishment learning (Dillien et al., 2020), and reversal-learning deficits (Dargis et al., 2017). Our findings extend this literature by showing reduced reward-based learning and relatively greater reliance on punishmentbased learning in prisoners at follow-up. This pattern is consistent with alterations that may develop during incarceration rather than solely reflecting pre-existing characteristics, though the longitudinal change was not itself statistically established. Several limitations should be considered. First, the outgoing sample did not meet power requirements and may have reduced statistical power. Second, the follow-up period primarily captured early adaptation to incarceration rather than long-term consequences. Longer longitudinal studies are needed to determine whether these alterations persist, strengthen, or normalize over time. Third, the control group was not demographically matched to the prisoner groups. However, the primary purpose of the control group was to quantify practice effects rather than to serve as a matched comparison sample. Moreover, no baseline difference was observed in the computational parameters, and supplementary analyses indicated that age was not consistently associated with the RL parameters. Nevertheless, because the computational models were fitted separately within each Group × Session dataset, demographic and prison-related characteristics could not be incorporated directly, so residual confounding cannot be completely excluded. Finally, high attrition rates may have introduced a bias in the results, due to self-selection of participants with different characteristics. However, rather than participant disengagement, it was largely driven by practical follow-up constraints, such as anticipated release in incoming participants and difficulties reaching out to the outgoing participants. Such challenges are common in longitudinal studies involving incarcerated populations, where maintaining contact after release is inherently difficult (Quinn et al., 2018; Stewart et al., 2021). Future work should integrate institutional and psychosocial variables directly into hierarchical RL models to identify which aspects of incarceration drive changes in learning and neural feedback processing (Schurr et al., 2024).

This study provides the first longitudinal investigation combining computational RL modelling with EEG measures to examine adaptation to incarceration. Together, these findings suggest a shift in the relative weighting of positive and negative outcomes during value updating, with reduced reward-related learning alongside relatively greater reliance on punishment information. In parallel, enhanced delta responses further indicated altered neural outcome evaluation in prisoners. The findings are broadly consistent with theories proposing that restrictive and unpredictable environments promote vigilance towards negative outcomes and increase reliance on punishment-based learning (Browning et al., 2015; Robinson et al., 2013). Such adaptations may be functional within prison environments, where mistakes can carry substantial consequences and opportunities for reward are comparatively limited (Haney, 2012; Zamble & Porporino, 1988). However, if these alterations persist beyond incarceration, they may influence adaptive decision-making during community re-entry and thereby affect successful reintegration. These findings highlight the importance of combining computational and neurophysiological approaches to understand how restrictive environments shape learning and decision-making processes beyond what can be inferred from behaviour alone.

Materials and Methods

Participants

Participants were recruited as part of a larger longitudinal project investigating the effects of incarceration on executive functioning and decision-making. The study protocol was preregistered on the Open Science Framework (OSF; DOI: https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/596QD) and approved by the Ethics Committee of Ghent University (2023-085A). All participants provided written informed consent prior to participation.

Three groups were included: newly incarcerated prisoners (Incoming), prisoners approaching release (Outgoing), and community controls. Incoming and outgoing participants were recruited from 15 Belgian penitentiary institutions. Incoming and outgoing participants are collectively referred to as prisoners. Community controls (first-year psychology students) were included to estimate practice effects. Details regarding recruitment procedures and power analyses are provided in Appendix 1. Incoming participants were assessed within four to seven weeks following incarceration (M = 31.4 days, SD = 13.9). Outgoing participants were assessed shortly before release (M = 19.7 days, SD = 35.0). Communication with prisoners was facilitated by prison staff to arrange appointments. To minimize perceived links between the study and the judicial system, informed consent procedures were conducted without prison staff present and participants were explicitly informed that the research team operated independently from both prison authorities and the justice system. All eligible candidates received oral and written information regarding the study and provided written informed consent. Eligibility criteria and retention strategies are reported in Appendices 2 and 3.

Participants were reminded that they could withdraw from the study at any time without consequences. Incoming and outgoing participants completed behavioural tasks with EEG recordings, and questionnaires and received financial compensation (€10 for questionnaires and €10 for experimental tasks). Control participants completed the behavioural tasks and received course credits.

The final behavioural sample comprised 128 participants with usable data at both time points, including 42 controls, 61 incoming and 25 outgoing participants. The final EEG sample included 96 participants with usable data, comprising 31 controls, 46 incoming and 19 outgoing participants. Descriptive statistics for demographic and incarceration-related variables are reported in Appendix 4. Control participants were significantly younger than both incoming and outgoing participants, and incoming participants were significantly younger than outgoing participants (all ps < .001). Groups also differed in gender composition as community controls were predominantly female (34 female, 8 male), whereas incoming and outgoing participants were exclusively male.

Procedure and Materials Controls were tested in testing rooms at Ghent University. Sessions took place in institutional rooms in prison and at participants' homes or other locations at follow-up. Each session lasted approximately two hours and included five cognitive tasks, including the Bandit task reported here, administered during EEG recording, followed by questionnaires. Task order was randomized across participants to minimize fatigue and order effects.

Bandit Task

Participants completed a probabilistic two-armed bandit task adapted from Aylward et al. (2019) programmed in PsychoPy (Python 3.10). Participants were informed that their performance would determine a real monetary bonus constrained between €0 and €3. On each trial they chose between two slot machines with dynamically changing reward probabilities and received feedback (+€0.25 or -€0.25) after a jittered interval (Fig. 1). The task consisted of five blocks of 25 trials (125 trials total) and lasted approximately 15 minutes.

Reward contingencies were nonstationary and changed across blocks. One bandit was associated with a higher reward probability (~70% vs ~30%) and alternated between successive blocks. To introduce uncertainty, trial-specific reward probabilities were sampled around these block-specific means (SD = .10 for block means; SD = .05 for individual trial probabilities). Computational modelling was used to estimate behavioural outcomes.

Computational Modelling

Trial-level choices and outcomes were fit with hierarchical Bayesian RL models in hBayesDM (Stan), separately for each Group × Session to avoid cross-group pooling (Ahn et al., 2017; Aylward et al., 2019). Models were compared by leave-one-out information criterion (LOOIC).

The best-fitting model (banditNarm_lapse) included separate reward and punishment learning rates and sensitivities, governing how quickly and how strongly gains and losses updated option values, and a lapse parameter capturing attentional lapses, with choices following a softmax rule. Group-level posteriors were compared with 95% highest-densityinterval (HDI) contrasts (between groups within each session, and S2-S1 change within and between groups), with contrasts excluding zero considered credible. Full model specifications and fit are in Appendix 5. Supplementary analyses examined associations between age and individual-level parameter estimates within each Group × Session (Appendix 6).

EEG recording and processing

EEG was recorded using a 32-channel BioSemi ActiveTwo system arranged according to the international 10-20 layout and sampled at 2048 Hz. Preprocessing was performed in MNE-Python (Gramfort et al., 2013). Briefly, bad channels and noisy segments were identified visually. Power line noise was removed using notch filters at 50 Hz and harmonics. Independent Component Analysis (ICA) was used for automatic artifacts removal.

Components were classified using ICLabel (Li et al., 2022; Pion-Tonachini et al., 2019). Those classified as artifact (muscle, eye, heart, line noise, or channel noise) were removed, and components labelled as brain or other with a probability ≥ .70 were retained. Bad channels were interpolated, and data were re-referenced offline using the reference electrode standardization technique (REST; Yao, 2001; D. Yao et al., 2019). Event-related potential (ERP) and time-frequency representation (TFR) analyses were performed on feedback-locked epochs. Cleaned data were filtered between 0.5 and 40 Hz for ERP analyses and between 1 and 40 Hz for TFR, segmented from -2 to 2 s around feedback onset. Analyses focused on a frontocentral region of interest (FC1, FC2, Fz, Cz). Trials exceeding an interquartile rangebased threshold (±2 × IQR) on peak-to-peak amplitude, mean activity, or slope were rejected (13.2% for ERPs, 11.3% for time-frequency). FRN amplitude was quantified as the mean-peakto-mean-peak difference between the P2 (150-220 ms) and the subsequent negativity (220-300 ms), relative to a -300-0 ms baseline. Single-trial theta (3.5-8 Hz) and delta (1-3.5 Hz) power were extracted 0.1-0.7 s post-feedback from Morlet time-frequency representations z-scored to a -1-0 s baseline (Fig. 2). The full preprocessing procedure is in Appendix 7. baseline. Dashed rectangles denote the a priori delta (1-3.5 Hz, black) and theta (3.5-8 Hz, red) windows of interest (0.1-0.7 s) used to extract the trial-level power values analysed in the main text.

Statistical analyses

Because participant groups differed in age, age was included as a covariate in all analyses of behavioural performance and EEG outcomes. Optimal-choice probability was analysed with a binomial generalized linear mixed model (GLMM). Trial-level FRN, FMθ and delta amplitudes were analysed using linear mixed-effects models (LMM). Models included fixed effects of Group, Session, Block (behaviour) or Group, Session, Block and Outcome (EEG), and all interactions, with age entered as a centred covariate. Participant-level random intercepts and random slopes for Session, Block, and Outcome (for EEG) were included. Type-III omnibus tests used sum-to-zero contrasts (Wald χ² for the GLMM, Satterthwaite F for the LMMs).

Planned contrasts were computed from estimated marginal means (EMM) with Tukey correction (emmeans package).

Prior to modelling, trial-level EEG measures were screened for within-participant outliers using a two-of-three rule for the three following criteria: |z| > 3, MAD > 3, or beyond 1.5 × IQR, excluding ~1% of FRN, ~5% of theta, and ~5% of delta trials. Sensitivity analyses retaining all trials confirmed that the results were largely robust to outlier removal (Appendix 8). Full details on statistical procedure and model specifications are included in Appendix 8. In addition, because the control group comprised both women and men whereas all incarcerated participants were men, supplementary sensitivity analyses were conducted within the control group to evaluate potential effects of gender on behavioural, computational and neural outcomes. These analyses yielded no significant gender effects (all ps > .16; Appendix 10). Finally, to verify that the randomization of task order across participants did not affect the results, an additional robustness analysis was conducted.

Although task order was associated with overall behavioural performance (χ²(5) = 14.93, p = .011), including it as a covariate left all group, session, and interaction effects on every behavioural and neural outcome unchanged (Appendix 10). This research was supported by the IOP grant (BOF/IOP/2022/084) from Ghent University. 479

SI Appendix 1. Recruitment Procedures and Power Analysis

Recruitment and Eligibility Criteria. Participants were recruited across 15 penitentiary establishments in Belgium that varied in security level, detention regime, and capacity.

Recruitment periods differed across institutions due to practical constraints in obtaining access, primarily related to overcrowding and recurrent disruptions within the Belgian prison system. In particular, data collection was interrupted in April 2024 because of prolonged nationwide prison staff strikes, and access to several establishments remained intermittently restricted throughout the recruitment period. Recruitment periods and characteristics of participating institutions are provided in Supplementary Table S1-1.

The study formed part of a larger longitudinal project examining the effects of incarceration on executive functioning, decision-making, and social cognition. Given the substantial attrition typically observed in longitudinal prison research (Dirkzwager & Nieuwbeerta, 2018; Gonçalves et al., 2016; Hassan et al., 2011; Lennox et al., 2013), large baseline samples were targeted. Specifically, the project aimed to recruit approximately 300 incoming prisoners, 300 outgoing prisoners, and 60 community controls at baseline. The target sample size was informed by several a priori power analyses conducted using G*Power (Faul et al., 2007). First, analyses focusing exclusively on incarcerated participants used a mixed repeated-measures design with two groups (incoming vs. outgoing) and six repeated measurements, corresponding to the combination of the three-level Session factor and the two-level Feedback factor (win vs. loss). Assuming a medium effect size (f = .25) and 95% statistical power, the required sample size was estimated at 28 participants (14 per group). Second, analyses including the community control group to evaluate practice effects used a mixed repeated-measures design with three groups and four repeated measurements, corresponding to the two-level Session factor and two-level Feedback factor. Under identical assumptions, the required sample size was estimated at 45 participants (15 per group).

Finally, because the broader project focused primarily on longitudinal within-person change across three assessment waves, an additional power analysis was conducted separately within each detainee group. Assuming three sessions and two experimental conditions, this analysis indicated a required sample size of 28 participants per detainee group. Consequently, substantially larger baseline samples were recruited to ensure adequate statistical power following expected attrition.

The prison distribution of participants included in the final S1-S2 completer sample is presented in Supplementary Figure 1-1. Incoming participants were primarily recruited from Brugge (32.2%), Nieuw Dendermonde (27.1%), and Jamioulx (18.6%), with smaller proportions from Tournai (8.5%), Marche-en-Famenne (6.8%), Turnhout (5.1%), and Huy (1.7%). Outgoing participants were mainly recruited from Ittre (20.0%), Leuven Centraal (20.0%), and Leuze (16.0%), followed by Beveren (12.0%) and Saint-Hubert (12.0%). Smaller proportions were recruited from Brugge, Hoogstraten, Jamioulx, Marche-en-Famenne, and Ruiselede (each 4.0%).

Sample-size estimation (deviation from preregistration).

The preregistered sample-size target was derived from an a priori power analysis in G*Power (medium effect, f = .25, 95% power). However, G*Power approximates such designs as repeated-measures ANOVAs and misspecifies interactions that combine between-and within-subject factors (Brysbaert, 2019), and confirmatory analyses used (generalised) linear mixed-effects models. Therefore, an additional simulation-based power analysis for the design actually used was conducted. This analysis was not preregistered and was carried out after data collection. To avoid circularity, the effect size was set a priori by convention rather than from the observed estimates. For each candidate sample size, datasets were simulated from a binomial mixed-effects model with the full Group × Session × Feedback structure, a by-participant random intercept, and random slopes for Session and Feedback. Each participant contributed 125 trials per session, aggregated to binomial counts within each participant × session × feedback cell. The highestorder interaction was tested with a likelihood-ratio test comparing the full model against a model retaining all lower-order (two-way) interactions, and power was defined as the proportion of 500 simulated datasets in which this interaction was detected at α = .05. The cohort design evaluated included three groups × two sessions. Assuming a small interaction (odds ratio = 1.5) and moderate between-participant variability in the session change (random-slope SD = 0.4 on the logit scale), 85% power for the highest-order interaction was reached at approximately 30 participants per group. Main effects and lower-order interactions reached 80% power at smaller samples. The achieved samples met this target for incoming participants (n = 61 with complete behavioural data; n = 46 with usable EEG) and for controls (n = 42 and n = 31, respectively), but fell below it for outgoing participants (n = 25 and n = 19, respectively). Because the outgoing group was under-powered, its posterior contrasts are correspondingly wide, and null or near-zero effects in this group are interpreted with caution rather than as evidence of absence.
