SI Appendix 2. Inclusion and Exclusion Criteria

Participants were excluded if they had insufficient understanding of Dutch, French, or English, experienced visual or motor impairments, or exhibited inappropriate behaviour towards the researchers. Participants were also excluded if they had already been approached during a previous incarceration period. Initially, mental disorders were not an exclusion criterion.

However, participants who experienced active psychosis during testing, who were transferred to a psychiatric unit, who were unable to complete the task, or who were non-compliant were excluded. In rare cases, prison staff denied participation for security or disciplinary reasons.

Additional eligibility criteria differed by cohort. Incoming participants were additionally excluded if their incarceration was expected to be transient (e.g., imminent release, extradition, or deportation). In outgoing participants, follow-up eligibility depended on projected release timing. For two outgoing participants, release was delayed by up to 6 months and the second assessment was conducted in prison. For these participants, only the first measure was retained for baseline and they were tested twice outside prison, at 4 and 8 months after release. Outgoing participants released under a limited detention regime were excluded due to the hybrid nature of this status, which combines both incarceration and community reintegration. Outgoing participants were additionally required to have served at least six months in detention prior to the baseline assessment.

SI Appendix 3. Retention strategies

To facilitate longitudinal follow-up, participants provided contact details on paper-based forms. Comprehensive tracing details included participants' residential address, mobile phone number, email address, and social media profiles. When possible, participants also provided contact details for alternative contacts (e.g., relatives, friends, social workers, or services they anticipated contacting upon release). Similar strategies are commonly used in longitudinal studies involving marginalized or high-risk populations to ensure effective followup (Aitken et al., 2008; Stewart et al., 2021; Teague et al., 2018). Researchers ensured that participants understood how their secondary contacts' details would be used and that sensitive information, such as their criminal history, would not be disclosed to these individuals.

Several retention strategies were employed to maintain participant engagement throughout the follow-up period. Participants still in prison were contacted through institutional channels, whereas released participants were contacted using the information provided at baseline. When possible, participants transferred to another prison or re-incarcerated during follow-up were traced through the national penitentiary database (SIDIS) and reassessed in their new institution. Participants could withdraw at any time without consequences.

For participants remaining incarcerated

Incoming participants were notified of upcoming follow-up sessions through letters sent via the prison communication system (Prison Cloud) or via institutional intermediaries. This allowed participants who were employed within the prison to request time off or schedule testing outside of working hours. During ongoing recruitment periods, participants eligible for follow-up were also contacted directly on recruitment days to confirm their continued interest and availability. Participants transferred to a different penitentiary institution were identified through the SIDIS penitentiary database, and authorization to conduct testing in the new institution was obtained from the Belgian Federal Public Service Justice (Direction générale EPI -Établissements pénitentiaires).

For participants released from prison

Participants who were scheduled for release within three weeks following baseline testing were asked to provide contact information, including a telephone number, email address, and/or residential address. When available, contact details of a social worker, community center, or health service were also recorded. At approximately 3 months post-release, participants were contacted to schedule follow-up assessments. If initial contact attempts were unsuccessful, up to three follow-up messages were sent. For participants who could not be reached through the provided contact information, secondary contacts were used to obtain updated contact details when available. Letters were also sent to participants' last known residential address. Participants who were re-incarcerated during the follow-up period were identified through the SIDIS database, provided they had consented to this monitoring in the informed consent procedure. Participants who were re-incarcerated before S2 were excluded from follow-up analyses, whereas those re-incarcerated between S2 and S3 remained eligible for the final assessment.

Follow-up participation

For both incarcerated and released participants, they were considered lost to follow-up if they cancelled or were absent from two consecutive scheduled appointments, or did not respond to contact attempts for three consecutive weeks. However, participants who contacted the research team within six months after release were allowed to re-enter the study if participation remained feasible. Detailed participation rates and attrition at each follow-up wave are reported in Supplementary Figure 3-1.

SI Appendix 4. Descriptive Stats

The present analyses focused on the first two assessment waves ( S1 and S2), separated by approximately four months. Mean S1-S2 retest intervals were 128.2 days (SD = 17.4) for incoming participants, 170.9 days (SD = 77.0) for outgoing participants, and 119.9 days (SD = 10.3) for controls. The larger variability in the outgoing group primarily reflected logistical challenges associated with postrelease follow-up.

Age

A one-way analysis of variance (ANOVA) revealed a significant effect of group on age, F(2, 125) = 68.71, p < .001, η² = .524, indicating that age differed substantially across groups.

Control participants were significantly younger than both incoming participants (mean difference = 15.91 years, p < .001, d = 1.82) and outgoing participants (mean difference = 23.83 years, p < .001, d = 2.73). Incoming participants were also significantly younger than outgoing participants (mean difference = 7.92 years, p < .001, d = 0.91). Given these differences, age was included as a covariate in all behavioural performance and EEG statistical analyses. For the reinforcement-learning analyses, supplementary analyses (S6) were conducted to evaluate potential associations between age and model parameters.

Education Level

Education level was described only for the incoming and outgoing detainee groups because all control participants were university students. Among incoming participants, 6.8% reported no formal education, 23.7% primary education, 47.5% secondary education, and 22.0% higher education. Among outgoing participants, 12.0% reported no formal education, 24.0% primary education, 52.0% secondary education, and 12.0% higher education. Overall, both groups were characterised predominantly by secondary-level education (Supplementary Figure 4-1).

Sentence Length and Conviction Status

Sentence length distributions and conviction status are presented in Supplementary Figure 4-2. Among incoming participants, 45.8% were in remand or pre-trial detention, 1.7% reported sentences shorter than one year, 11.9% reported sentences between one and three years, 22.0% between three and five years, and 16.9% between five and ten years. Among outgoing participants, 68.0% reported sentences between five and ten years, 24.0% reported sentences between three and five years, 4.0% reported sentences shorter than one year, and 4.0% reported sentences between one and three years. Importantly, prisoners who had already been convicted and received sentences shorter than one year were generally not targeted during recruitment in order to reduce attrition risk across follow-up assessments.

Consequently, individuals serving very short sentences were underrepresented in the incoming sample.

Supp.

Previous Incarcerations

Previous incarceration was common in both groups (Supplementary Figure 4-3). Among incoming participants, 39.0% reported that the current detention was their first incarceration, 30.5% reported one previous incarceration, and 30.5% reported two to four previous incarcerations. Among outgoing participants, 32.0% reported no prior incarceration, 28.0% reported one prior incarceration, and 40.0% reported two to four previous incarcerations.

These proportions are consistent with the high rates of re-incarceration in Belgium and indicate that repeated contact with the prison system was common in both detainee groups.

Types of Offences

Participants frequently reported multiple offence categories. Consequently, percentages shown in Supplementary Figure 4-4 represent the proportion of participants reporting each offence type rather than mutually exclusive categories. Among incoming participants, the most frequently reported offence categories were drug-related offences (32.2%), non-violent property offences (30.5%), violent offences (27.1%), sexual offences (13.6%), and offences involving arms possession (10.2%). Violent property offences were reported by 10.2% of incoming participants, whereas life offences accounted for 8.5% of reports. Among outgoing participants, the most frequently reported offence categories were drug-related offences (36.0%), non-violent property offences (28.0%), sexual offences (28.0%), violent offences (24.0%), and life offences (24.0%). Traffic offences were reported by 20.0% of outgoing participants, whereas arms-related and violent property offences were each reported by 12.0%. parameters. It tracks the value of each option separately for rewards and punishments and updates these values trial by trial as outcomes are received. Learning from rewards and from punishments was governed by separate learning rates (Arew and Apun), and the impact of each reward and punishment was scaled by separate sensitivity parameters (R and P). Choices were modelled with a softmax rule applied to the combined reward and punishment values, and a lapse parameter (ξ) allowed a proportion of responses to be made at random, independently of learned value. Learning rates and the lapse parameter were bounded between 0 and 1, and the reward and punishment sensitivities between 0 and 30. Models that omitted separate reward and punishment sensitivities or included only learning-rate parameters performed substantially worse.

Posterior group-level parameter distributions from the winning model were compared using 95% highest density interval (HDI) contrasts. For each comparison, posterior samples were subtracted to obtain a distribution of parameter differences, from which the 95% HDI was calculated. Between-group contrasts were computed separately within each session, longitudinal changes were estimated as S2 -S1 contrasts within each group, and group differences in longitudinal change were evaluated using contrasts of posterior session effects, analogous to a Bayesian difference-in-differences approach. Following Kruschke (2014) and Aylward et al. (2019), a contrast was considered meaningful when the 95% HDI excluded zero.

The full between-group and longitudinal posterior contrasts for the winning model are reported in Tables S5-4 and S5-5, respectively.

To confirm that the parameters were adequately constrained under the default priors, we examined the individual posterior-mean estimates and the group-level standard deviations (σ) for each parameter and dataset (Table S5-3). All estimates fell within the model's bounds, with no accumulation at boundary values. Between-subject spread differed markedly across parameters. The reward learning rate (Arew), reward sensitivity (R) and punishment sensitivity (P) showed substantial individual variation, whereas the punishment learning rate (Apun) and lapse (ξ) were strongly shrunk toward the group mean (across-subject SD ≈ 0.004-0.02). Because Arew lies on the same (0, 1) scale as Apun and ξ yet varied widely (≈ 0.05-0.95), this narrow spread reflects weak individual-level identifiability as there were few loss trials to inform Apun, and lapse estimates lie near zero. Individual differences in Apun and ξ should therefore be interpreted with caution. Inference for these parameters is most reliable at the group level.

The strong shrinkage of Apun and ξ (across-subject SD ≈ 0.004-0.02) reflects weak individual-level identifiability, with reliable inference at the group level.

SI Appendix 7. EEG recording and preprocessing

EEG was recorded during task performance using a 32-channel BioSemi ActiveTwo system arranged according to the international 10-20 system and sampled at 2048 Hz. External electrodes were used to record mastoid activity. Raw EEG data were acquired with ActiveView software and converted to BIDS format. EEG preprocessing was conducted in MNE-Python (Gramfort et al., 2013).

Raw files were first inspected to verify recording quality. For ICA-based artefact correction, a copy of the raw data was band-pass filtered between 1 and 30 Hz using an IIR filter. Powerline noise was attenuated using notch filters at 50 Hz and harmonics up to 250 Hz. Independent Component Analysis was then applied using previously estimated ICA decompositions, and components were classified using ICLabel (Li et al., 2022; Pion-Tonachini et al., 2019). Components classified as artefact (muscle, eye, heart, line noise, or channel noise) were removed, and components labelled as brain or "other" with a probability ≥ .70 were retained, following the ICLabel developers' recommendation not to discard "other" components, which may still contain brain activity (Pion-Tonachini et al., 2019). Comparable ICLabel probability thresholds (.70-.80) are documented in standard pipelines (MNE-BIDS-Pipeline) and have been applied in prior EEG work (Amacker et al., 2026; Asogbon et al., 2023).

Bad channels were interpolated, and the cleaned data were re-referenced using the reference electrode standardization technique, implemented with a spherical head model and forward solution (REST; Yao, 2001; D. Yao et al., 2019). ERP and time-frequency analyses were performed on feedback-locked epochs on frontocentral electrodes Fz, Cz, FC1 and FC2. These sites were selected because feedback-related frontal-midline activity is maximal over medial frontocentral cortex. Indeed, the feedback-related negativity (FRN) and feedback-locked theta and delta responses consistently peak at these electrodes, consistent with a putative generator in the anterior cingulate/medial frontal cortex (Cavanagh & Frank, 2014; Cohen, 2014; Holroyd & Coles, 2002; Sambrook & Goslin, 2015).

For ERP analyses, cleaned continuous data were band-pass filtered between 0.5 and 40 Hz and segmented into epochs from -2 to 2 s relative to feedback onset. Epochs were baselinecorrected using the -300 to 0 ms pre-feedback interval and cropped from -300 to 600 ms for analysis. Automated epoch rejection was applied over the -1.5 to 1 s window using the electrodes of interest only. Trials were rejected when identified as outliers on peak-to-peak amplitude, mean amplitude, or signal slope. For each participant and electrode, trials exceeding an interquartile-range threshold (±2 × IQR) on any metric were rejected. On average, 13.2% of ERP epochs were rejected, with comparable rates across groups (CTRL: 11.4%; IN: 14.8%; OUT: 12.5%). Participants were retained only if they contributed at least 10 valid trials per outcome feedback and at least 30 feedback trials overall. The FRN was quantified over frontocentral electrodes Fz, FC1, FC2, and Cz using the mean peak-to-mean peak measure. For each trial, activity was averaged across available electrodes in this region of interest. The analysis window was defined from the grand-average waveform collapsed across groups, sessions, and outcome feedbacks. The preceding positive peak (P2) was identified within the 150-220 ms window and quantified as the mean amplitude in a ±20 ms window around it. The FRN local minimum was identified within the 220-300 ms window and quantified as the mean amplitude in a ±20 ms window around it. Trial-level FRN amplitude was computed as the FRN-window mean minus the P2-window mean, such that more negative values indicate a stronger downward deflection relative to the preceding positivity.

For time-frequency analyses, cleaned continuous data were filtered between 1 and 40 Hz and segmented into feedback-locked epochs from -2 to 2 s. Time-frequency representations were computed at the single-trial level using Morlet wavelets with 40 logarithmically spaced frequencies from 1 to 40 Hz, with the number of cycles increasing logarithmically from 1 to 14. Power was z-score baseline-normalized relative to the -1 to 0 s pre-feedback interval and cropped to -2 to 2 s. Time-frequency windows were defined from the grand-average activity collapsed across groups, sessions, and outcome feedbacks. Analyses focused on frontocentral electrodes Fz, FC1, FC2, and Cz. Delta power was extracted between 1 and 3.5 Hz and theta power between 3.5 and 8 Hz, both from 100 to 700 ms after feedback onset. Automated epoch rejection for the time-frequency epochs used the same procedure as for the ERP analyses. On average, 11.3% of time-frequency epochs used for delta and theta were rejected across sessions (CTRL: 10.9%; IN: 11.7%; OUT: 11.0%).

SI Appendix 5. Computational Modelling

Computational modelling was performed in R using the hBayesDM package (Ahn et al., 2017), which implements hierarchical Bayesian parameter estimation in Stan. Models were fitted to trial-by-trial choice and outcome data from the probabilistic bandit task. For each model, three Markov chain Monte Carlo chains were run for 4,000 iterations per chain, including 1,000 burn-in iterations. All models were estimated using the default priors implemented in hBayesDM, with each parameter transformed to its bounded scale. Seven reinforcementlearning models were evaluated: bandit2arm_delta, banditNarm_2par_lapse, banditNarm_4par, banditNarm_delta, banditNarm_lapse, banditNarm_lapse_decay, and banditNarm_singleA_lapse (Supplementary Table S5-1). These models differed in the number of free parameters and assumptions regarding learning from reward and punishment outcomes, choice stochasticity, and decay of unchosen option values. Depending on model specification, parameters included reward sensitivity (R), punishment sensitivity (P), reward learning rate (Arew), punishment learning rate (Apun), lapse rate (ξ), and decay. Following Aylward et al. (2019), behavioural data from each Group (Control, Incoming, Outgoing) and Session (S1, S2) were initially fitted separately using independent hierarchical priors. This approach minimizes hierarchical shrinkage across groups and sessions, allowing potential between-group and longitudinal differences to emerge without partial pooling. To assess the robustness of findings to prior specification, the best-performing models were subsequently re-estimated using alternative hierarchical prior structures based on pooled datasets across (a) groups, (b) sessions, and (c) groups and sessions simultaneously.

Model comparison was based on the leave-one-out information criterion (LOOIC). Lower

LOOIC values indicate better expected out-of-sample predictive performance. The winning model was identified as the model with the lowest average LOOIC across the six group-bysession datasets (Control S1, Control S2, Incoming S1, Incoming S2, Outgoing S1, and Outgoing S2). Model comparison indicated that the banditNarm_lapse model provided the best overall fit to the data (Supplementary Table S5-2), and was therefore retained for subsequent analyses. The two best models were within ~1 LOOIC (indistinguishable given the SE of the difference), so the lapse model was retained for its theoretically motivated valueindependent responding term. The retained model, banditNarm_lapse, is a reinforcementlearning model for the N-armed bandit adapted from Seymour et al. (2012), with five free

SI Appendix 6. Age Effects on Computational Model Parameters

To evaluate whether age was associated with reinforcement-learning parameters, individuallevel parameter estimates from the winning model were extracted separately for each Group × Session dataset and merged with participants' age. Because the hierarchical reinforcementlearning models were estimated independently for each Group × Session, these analyses were restricted to within-dataset associations and were not used to compare age effects across groups or sessions. For each parameter, linear models tested the association between age and individual-level estimates within each Group × Session. Age was centred within each Group × Session, and false-discovery-rate correction was applied across tests.

Age was not consistently associated with punishment learning rate, punishment sensitivity, or reward learning rate across Group × Session datasets (all FDR-corrected ps > .05).

Significant associations were observed for reward sensitivity in Control S1, Control S2, Incoming S1, and Outgoing S2, and for lapse in Control S1, Control S2, and Outgoing S1 (Supplementary Table S6-1).

These associations were inconsistent across groups and sessions and therefore did not correspond to the longitudinal pattern observed in the primary HDI analyses. Overall, age did not show a consistent association with the computational parameters across Group × Session datasets.

SI Appendix 8. Statistical analyses

To complement the computational modelling results (Supplementary Material S5), trial-level behavioural performance was analysed using a generalized linear mixed-effects model (GLMM) implemented in the lme4 package. The dependent variable was whether the optimal bandit was selected on each trial (0 = non-optimal,1 = optimal). Fixed effects included Group, Session, Block, and all interactions, with age entered as a centred covariate. Participantspecific random intercepts and random slopes for Session and Block were included:

This analysis evaluated whether optimal responding changed across successive contingency reversals and whether these learning trajectories differed between groups across sessions.

Feedback-locked FRN amplitude was analysed using linear mixed-effects models (LMM) implemented in the lme4 and lmerTest packages. The model included fixed effects of Group, Session, Outcome (Reward vs. Punishment), and all interactions, with age as a covariate.

Participant-specific random intercepts and random slopes for Session and Outcome were included:

Single-trial theta and delta power were analysed using LMM with identical fixed-and randomeffects structures:

Prior to modelling, trial-level EEG measures (FRN amplitude, theta power, and delta power) were screened for within-participant outliers using a two-of-three rule combining three criteria: z-score (|z| > 3), median absolute deviation (MAD > 3), and interquartile range (values beyond 1.5 × IQR).

A trial was excluded when flagged by at least two of the three criteria. This removed approximately 1% of FRN trials, 5% of theta trials, and 5% of delta trials.

Model assumptions were evaluated through convergence diagnostics, singular-fit checks, and visual inspection of residual and fitted-value distributions. Estimated marginal means and planned contrasts were computed using the emmeans package. The primary contrasts tested longitudinal S1-S2 changes separately within each Group × Outcome combination. Betweengroup comparisons within each Session × Outcome were treated as secondary contrasts, whereas Outcome (Reward-Punishment) contrasts within each Group × Session combination served as task-validity checks. Tukey adjustment was applied for multiple comparisons within each family of planned contrasts.

To assess robustness to outlier removal, the FRN, theta, and delta models were refitted with all trials retained. Of 24 fixed-effect tests and 60 planned contrasts, 23 and 52 were unchanged in significance. All FRN effects and delta-power group effects were robust. For theta, the only reported effect that did not survive was the outgoing group's S2 win-lose differentiation (p = .041 without outliers vs. p = .098 with outliers). The remaining changes involved contrasts that were non-significant in the main analysis and reached significance only when outliers were retained, indicating that outlier removal yielded a more conservative pattern of results.

SI Appendix 10. Sensitivity Analyses Gender

Because the control group comprised both women and men (34 F and 8 M) whereas all incarcerated participants were men, we could not include gender as a covariate in the full between-group models without confounding it with group. We therefore evaluated gender effects within the control group only, entering gender as a covariate in each behavioural, neural, and computational model. Gender did not significantly predict any outcome (all ps > .16; Supplementary Table S10-1). We therefore retained the gender-imbalanced control group without adjustment in the main analyses.

Table S10-1. Control-only gender effects. Outcome p (GenderM) Optimal choice .95 Final outcome .69 FRN amplitude .44 Frontal-midline θ power .48 Delta power .35 Reward sensitivity (R) .72 Punishment sensitivity (P) .17 Reward learning rate (Arew) .27 Punishment learning rate (Apun) .92 Lapse (ξ) .75 Behavioural and neural effects tested with (G)LMMs (Satterthwaite / type-III Wald); computational parameters tested by regressing individual posterior-mean estimates on gender and session.

Task order

Although task order was randomised across participants, we verified that it did not confound the group or longitudinal effects. For each outcome we tested whether task order improved model fit and whether the group, session, outcome, and interaction effects changed when task order was added as a covariate. Task order was associated with overall behavioural performance (χ²(5) = 14.93, p = .011) but not with any neural measure (FRN, FMθ, delta; all ps ≥ .36; Supplementary Table S10-2). Critically, including task order as a covariate left every effect of interest unchanged (Supplementary Table S10-3). None of the 19 group, session, outcome, or interaction terms across the five outcomes changed significance, including the Group × Session × Block interaction on optimal choice (p ≤ .033 with and without task order) and the large group effects on the neural measures. Task order therefore influenced overall behavioural performance but did not drive the group or longitudinal effects.

Figure 1 :

Figure1: Overview of the probabilistic Bandit Task. Each trial began with a fixation cross (800-1200 ms), followed by the presentation of two slot machines. Participants had up to 4 s to choose one option. The selected slot machine remained on the screen for 1 s. After another fixation cross, feedback was presented for 1 s, indicating either a monetary gain (+€0.25), a monetary loss (-€0.25), or a missed response ("Too late!"). A second fixation cross (800-1200 ms) separated consecutive trials. The task consisted of five blocks of 25 trials, with reward contingencies reversing between blocks, requiring participants to learn and continuously update the most advantageous choice.

Shaded regions mark the pre-feedback baseline (-300-0 ms), the P2 window (150-220 ms), and the FRN window (220-300 ms); FRN amplitude was quantified as the mean-peak-to-mean-peak difference between the P2 and the subsequent FRN. Scalp topographies (top) show the grand-average voltage distribution at representative postfeedback latencies (P2: 160 and 200 ms; FRN: 250 ms; P3: 300 ms). Warmer colours indicate more positive voltages (µV). (B) Feedback-locked time-frequency representation of power at the same frontocentral electrodes. Power is expressed as a z-score relative to the -1.0 to 0 s pre-feedback baseline (dotted line marks baseline onset and solid line marks feedback onset at 0 s).
